1	# Milestone 4 Research Summary
2	
3	**Project:** CipherBox -- Encrypted Productivity Suite
4	**Domain:** Zero-knowledge document editing, team accounts, billing, document signing
5	**Researched:** 2026-02-11
6	**Confidence:** MEDIUM
7	
8	## Executive Summary
9	
10	Milestone 4 transforms CipherBox from an encrypted file locker into an encrypted productivity platform. The four feature pillars -- billing, team accounts, document editors, and document signing -- vary dramatically in complexity. Billing (Stripe + optional crypto payments) is straightforward with well-documented patterns. Team accounts introduce a real key hierarchy challenge but follow established models from Keybase and Tresorit. Document editors are the highest-risk, highest-effort feature: integrating TipTap + Yjs for rich text editing with encrypted CRDT transport is novel territory that CryptPad and Proton Docs each spent years building. Document signing is the simplest win, leveraging existing Web3Auth secp256k1 keys for ECDSA attestation.
11	
12	The recommended approach is to build in strict dependency order: Billing first (enables tier gating), then Teams (enables shared vaults), then Document Editors (the flagship feature), then Signing (additive to existing documents). This order minimizes rework -- each phase builds on the previous one. Spreadsheets should ship as single-user-editable only (no real-time collaboration), and slides should be deferred entirely. Real-time collaboration for documents should also be deferred to M5+; M4 should implement single-user editing with advisory locking for team documents.
13	
14	The critical risks center on two threats to the zero-knowledge guarantee: editor libraries leaking plaintext through collaboration protocols, and team key distribution introducing server-visible key material. Both are solvable with known patterns (encrypted CRDT transport, ECIES-wrapped per-team keys) but require deliberate architecture -- the path of least resistance in every editor and team library assumes a trusted server. Autosave conflicting with IPNS publish latency is a secondary but real risk that needs a save queue with mutual exclusion from day one.
15	
16	## Key Findings
17	
18	### Recommended Stack
19	
20	The stack extends CipherBox's existing NestJS + React + TypeORM + IPFS architecture. No foundational technology changes are needed -- M4 is additive.
21	
22	Core technologies:
23	
24	- **TipTap 3.x + Yjs** (rich text editing) -- Stable v3, ProseMirror-based, native Yjs CRDT support, MIT licensed, used by Proton Docs. HIGH confidence.
25	- **Univer 0.15.x** (spreadsheet editing) -- Best open-source spreadsheet engine, client-side formula computation, Apache 2.0. MEDIUM confidence (pre-1.0 API instability risk).
26	- **Hocuspocus 3.4.x** (collaboration relay) -- WebSocket relay for Yjs sync; must be used as a dumb encrypted pipe, not as a document-aware server.
27	- **CASL 6.x** (authorization) -- Standard RBAC/ABAC library for NestJS with React client-side integration. HIGH confidence.
28	- **Stripe 20.x + @golevelup/nestjs-stripe** (traditional billing) -- Industry standard, mature NestJS integration. HIGH confidence.
29	- **NOWPayments** (crypto billing) -- SaaS crypto payment processor, 350+ coins, no self-hosting required. MEDIUM confidence (SDK less actively maintained).
30	- **LibPDF** (document signing) -- TypeScript-first PDF library with PAdES digital signature support, built by Documenso. MEDIUM-LOW confidence (beta).
31	- **react-signature-canvas** (visual signature capture) -- Lightweight signature drawing pad. HIGH confidence.
32	
33	Critical version requirements:
34	
35	- TipTap must be v3.x (stable since Feb 2026, Yjs collaboration extensions only in v3)
36	- Univer at 0.15.x; expect breaking changes between minor versions
37	- LibPDF is beta -- have @signpdf/signpdf as a server-side fallback
38	
39	### Expected Features
40	
41	Must have (table stakes):
42	
43	- Free tier with storage limits and paid subscription tiers (Stripe Checkout + Customer Portal)
44	- Organization/team creation with member invitation and role-based permissions (owner/admin/editor/viewer)
45	- In-browser rich text document editing with basic formatting (bold, italic, headings, lists, links, tables)
46	- Per-document AES-256-GCM encryption using existing key hierarchy
47	- Autosave with debounce and dirty indicator
48	- Document signing with ECDSA using Web3Auth secp256k1 keys
49	- Signature verification by any party with signer's public key
50	- Visual signature capture (draw/type)
51	
52	Should have (differentiators):
53	
54	- Anonymous crypto payments (NOWPayments or BTCPay Server)
55	- IPFS-backed document storage with CID-based integrity verification
56	- Spreadsheet editing (Univer, single-user only)
57	- Multi-party signing workflows with signature request UI
58	- IPFS-anchored signatures with tamper-evident CID linking
59	- Client-side PDF export with embedded digital signature
60	- Advisory document locking for team contexts
61	
62	Defer to M4+:
63	
64	- Real-time collaborative editing (encrypted CRDT relay via WebSocket)
65	- Spreadsheet real-time collaboration (Univer uses OT, incompatible with ZK without custom Yjs adapter)
66	- Slide/presentation editing (no mature open-source WYSIWYG editor exists)
67	- Blockchain-timestamped signing (smart contract integration)
68	- Zero-knowledge organization metadata (encrypted org structure on IPFS)
69	- Cryptographic role enforcement (separate read/write key hierarchies)
70	- SSO/LDAP, enterprise policy templates
71	
72	### Architecture Approach
73	
74	M3 follows a "decrypt-edit-encrypt" pipeline pattern: files are fetched from IPFS, decrypted client-side, loaded into an in-browser editor, and re-encrypted on save. This is architecturally identical to the existing file update flow but replaces the download/re-upload step with an embedded editor. Team accounts extend the key hierarchy by introducing a Per-Team Key (PTK) that is ECIES-wrapped for each member's public key -- file keys within team folders are encrypted with the PTK, not individual member keys. Billing metadata (tier, quotas) is the only new server-side state; all user content remains in the zero-knowledge layer (IPNS metadata + IPFS content).
75	
76	Major components:
77	
78	1. **Editor module** (frontend) -- TipTap document editor, Univer spreadsheet editor, decrypt-edit-encrypt pipeline service, autosave with debounced save queue
79	2. **Teams module** (API + frontend) -- Team CRUD, member management, ECIES key wrapping per member, team vault initialization with team-scoped IPNS keypairs
80	3. **Billing module** (API + frontend) -- Stripe Checkout/Portal/webhooks, NOWPayments invoice/IPN webhooks, subscription entity with tier enforcement in NestJS guards
81	4. **Signing module** (frontend-only) -- ECDSA signing/verification via Web Crypto API, signature metadata stored in encrypted IPNS folder metadata, react-signature-canvas for visual capture
82	
83	### Critical Pitfalls
84	
85	1. **Editor plaintext leakage (C1)** -- All mainstream collaboration libraries (Hocuspocus, Liveblocks, TipTap Cloud) transmit plaintext by default. CRDT updates MUST be encrypted client-side before WebSocket transmission. Build a custom Yjs provider that encrypts with the document key. Audit every WebSocket frame in DevTools.
86	
87	2. **Team key distribution without trusted server (C2)** -- Team keys must be ECIES-wrapped per member, never stored in plaintext on server. Member removal MUST trigger key rotation (new PTK, re-wrap for remaining members). Model after Keybase's per-team-key architecture with key epochs.
88	
89	3. **Autosave vs. IPNS publish latency (C3)** -- Each save requires encrypt + IPFS upload + IPNS publish (2-12 seconds total). Implement a save queue with mutual exclusion; only one save in-flight at a time. Separate real-time editing state (local CRDT) from persistence (debounced IPNS snapshots at 10-30 second intervals).
90	
91	4. **Admin recovery that breaks ZK (C4)** -- Never store a server-side admin recovery key. Recovery requires an authenticated admin with their private key in client RAM. Accept that if all admins lose their keys, team data is unrecoverable. This is the correct security/convenience tradeoff for ZK.
92	
93	5. **Scope explosion from spreadsheets and slides (M2)** -- Spreadsheets are an order of magnitude harder than rich text. Proton launched Docs in mid-2024 and Sheets in late 2025. Slides have no mature open-source editor. Scope M3 to documents + basic spreadsheets only.
94	
95	## Implications for Roadmap
96	
97	Based on research, the suggested phase structure is four sequential phases following the dependency chain: Billing -> Teams -> Editors -> Signing.
98	
99	### Phase 1: Billing Infrastructure
100	
101	**Rationale:** Billing is independent of all other M3 features and has zero crypto complexity. The tier system gates access to team features and editor usage, so it must exist before other features can enforce limits. This is also the lowest-risk phase with the highest confidence stack (Stripe).
102	
103	**Delivers:** Free/Pro/Team subscription tiers, Stripe Checkout and Customer Portal, webhook-driven provisioning, subscription entity with quota enforcement, billing settings UI. Optionally: NOWPayments crypto payment integration.
104	
105	**Addresses features:** Free tier with storage limits, paid subscription tiers, Stripe Checkout, subscription management, invoices, webhook provisioning, grace period for failed payments.
106	
107	**Avoids pitfalls:** M1 (billing state desync) by implementing idempotent webhooks and periodic Stripe reconciliation from day one. M6 (webhook fraud) by requiring Stripe signature verification on all webhook endpoints.
108	
109	**Estimated effort:** 2-4 weeks.
110	
111	### Phase 2: Team Accounts
112	
113	**Rationale:** Team infrastructure must exist before team-aware editors and team document signing. The key hierarchy extension (Per-Team Key wrapping) is the most architecturally significant change in M3 and needs to be designed carefully before editors add complexity.
114	
115	**Delivers:** Team CRUD, member invitation with ECIES key wrapping, role-based permissions (owner/admin/editor/viewer via CASL), team vault initialization with team-scoped IPNS keypairs, team sidebar UI and vault switching, key rotation on member removal.
116	
117	**Addresses features:** Create organization, invite members, role-based permissions, team/group abstraction, remove member with key revocation, member device management.
118	
119	**Avoids pitfalls:** C2 (key distribution) by implementing ECIES-wrapped Per-Team Keys from the start. C4 (admin recovery) by designing recovery as admin-key-gated, never server-side. N2 (key wrapping scaling) by using two-level key hierarchy (PTK wraps file keys, member keys wrap PTK).
120	
121	**Estimated effort:** 4-6 weeks.
122	
123	### Phase 3: Document Editors
124	
125	**Rationale:** Editors are the highest-complexity, highest-visibility feature. They benefit from having teams in place so team members can test editing flows on shared documents. Single-user editing first, with advisory locking for team contexts. Real-time collaboration deferred to M4+.
126	
127	**Delivers:** TipTap rich text editor integration, Univer spreadsheet editor integration (single-user), decrypt-edit-encrypt pipeline service, autosave with debounced save queue, metadata schema extensions (editorType, editorFormat), "New Document" / "New Spreadsheet" creation flow, advisory locking for team documents, document export (PDF, Markdown, XLSX).
128	
129	**Addresses features:** In-browser document editing, basic rich text formatting, document-level encryption, offline single-user editing, document history/undo, copy/paste, export to common formats, IPFS-backed document storage.
130	
131	**Avoids pitfalls:** C1 (plaintext leakage) by not implementing real-time collaboration in M3 -- single-user editing eliminates the encrypted relay requirement entirely. C3 (autosave conflicts) by implementing a save queue with mutual exclusion. C5 (CRDT tombstone bloat) is deferred since real-time CRDTs are deferred. M3 (encryption performance) by using Web Workers for crypto operations and setting document size limits.
132	
133	**Estimated effort:** 6-10 weeks.
134	
135	### Phase 4: Document Signing
136	
137	**Rationale:** Signing is additive to documents that already exist. It is the simplest M3 feature and leverages existing Web3Auth secp256k1 keys. Building it last means documents and team contexts are stable.
138	
139	**Delivers:** ECDSA signing service (client-side via Web Crypto API), signature verification, visual signature capture (draw/type via react-signature-canvas), multi-signer workflows with signature request UI, signature status indicators in file browser, signed PDF export via LibPDF.
140	
141	**Addresses features:** Sign document with private key, verify signature, visual signature capture, multi-party signing workflow, audit trail, signed document export.
142	
143	**Avoids pitfalls:** M5 (legal validity) by labeling signatures as "cryptographic attestation" rather than "legally binding e-signature." N4 (certificate management) by skipping X.509 certificates and using the existing Web3Auth keypair directly.
144	
145	**Estimated effort:** 3-5 weeks.
146	
147	### Phase Ordering Rationale
148	
149	- Billing -> Teams: Team member limits and team tier pricing are gated by billing. Building billing first means teams can enforce seat limits from day one.
150	- Teams -> Editors: Editors need team vaults and shared folders to be meaningful in a multi-user context. Advisory locking for team documents requires the team permission model.
151	- Editors -> Signing: Signing requires a document to sign. Building editors first provides the document creation and viewing infrastructure that signing builds upon.
152	- Single-user editing in M3, real-time collaboration in M4: This is the most important scoping decision. Real-time encrypted CRDT collaboration is the highest-risk item in all of M3 research. Deferring it to M4 dramatically reduces risk and effort while still delivering the core editor experience.
153	
154	### Research Flags
155	
156	Phases likely needing deeper research during planning:
157	
158	- **Phase 2 (Teams):** Key rotation on member removal is complex. Need security review of the key epoch chain design. IPNS record size limits under team metadata growth (N members x M wrapped keys) need validation.
159	- **Phase 3 (Editors):** TipTap + Univer integration specifics, bundle size impact, mobile compatibility, autosave pipeline performance with IPFS/IPNS latency. A proof-of-concept of the decrypt-edit-encrypt pipeline should be built early.
160	
161	Phases with standard patterns (skip additional research):
162	
163	- **Phase 1 (Billing):** Stripe integration is well-documented with mature NestJS modules. Standard webhook patterns apply.
164	- **Phase 4 (Signing):** Uses existing ECDSA primitives (secp256k1 via Web3Auth + Web Crypto API). Straightforward implementation.
165	
166	## Confidence Assessment
167	
168	| Area         | Confidence  | Notes                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
169	| ------------ | ----------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
170	| Stack        | MEDIUM-HIGH | TipTap, Stripe, CASL all HIGH confidence. Univer (MEDIUM, pre-1.0) and LibPDF (MEDIUM-LOW, beta) pull the average down.                                                                                     |
171	| Features     | MEDIUM      | Feature landscape well-mapped via competitor analysis. Complexity estimates are informed approximations, not empirical.                                                                                     |
172	| Architecture | MEDIUM      | Billing and teams follow well-established ZK patterns (Keybase, Tresorit). Editor integration with encrypted IPFS/IPNS persistence is novel and untested at scale.                                          |
173	| Pitfalls     | MEDIUM-HIGH | Critical pitfalls (plaintext leakage, key distribution, autosave conflicts) are well-documented in academic and industry literature. Interaction with CipherBox-specific IPNS architecture is less certain. |
174	
175	**Overall confidence:** MEDIUM
176	
177	### Gaps to Address
178	
179	- **Encrypted CRDT performance budget:** What latency does encrypting/decrypting Yjs updates add? Is sub-100ms keystroke propagation achievable for M4 real-time collaboration? Needs benchmarking during Phase 3.
180	- **IPNS record size limits under team metadata:** As teams grow (50+ members with wrapped keys), do IPNS records hit practical size limits? Needs measurement during Phase 2.
181	- **Univer API stability:** Univer is pre-1.0 with frequent breaking changes. Pin the exact version during Phase 3 and budget for API migration if minor version updates break compatibility.
182	- **LibPDF maturity for PDF signing:** LibPDF is beta. If PAdES signature embedding is unreliable, fall back to @signpdf/signpdf on the server side (with the document hash, not plaintext, sent to server).
183	- **NOWPayments recurring API reliability:** Marketing claims subscription support, but battle-tested reports are scarce. Validate with a proof-of-concept before committing to crypto billing in Phase 1.
184	- **Offline editing with team key rotation:** If a team member edits offline while the team key is rotated, their local state is encrypted with the old key. Recovery path needs design during Phase 2.
185

1	# Feature Landscape: Milestone 4
2	
3	**Domain:** Encrypted productivity platform (docs, sheets, slides, teams, billing, signing)
4	**Researched:** 2026-02-11
5	**Confidence:** MEDIUM (grounded in competitor analysis; complexity estimates are informed approximations)
6	
7	## Context
8	
9	CipherBox already provides (M1) or will provide (M2) zero-knowledge encrypted file storage with IPFS/IPNS, Web3Auth, file/folder sharing, versioning, encrypted search, and vault export. Milestone 4 extends CipherBox from an encrypted file locker into a full encrypted productivity platform.
10	
11	The four feature pillars researched:
12	
13	1. Encrypted document editors (docs, sheets, slides)
14	2. Team accounts (organizations, roles, permissions)
15	3. Billing (Stripe + crypto/anonymous payments)
16	4. Secure document signing (e-signatures)
17	
18	---
19	
20	## Feature 1: Encrypted Document Editing
21	
22	### How It Works in Zero-Knowledge Systems
23	
24	The dominant pattern, used by CryptPad, Skiff (discontinued), and Proton Docs, is:
25	
26	1. **CRDT-based collaboration** -- Conflict-free Replicated Data Types allow multiple clients to make concurrent edits that converge without a central authority resolving conflicts. Since the server never needs to understand document content, all CRDT updates can be end-to-end encrypted before transmission.
27	
28	2. **Symmetric document key** -- Each document has a random AES-256 key. All collaborators hold this key (distributed via ECIES key-wrapping, same pattern CipherBox already uses for folder keys). CRDT patches are encrypted with this key before being relayed through the server.
29	
30	3. **Relay server** -- The server acts as a dumb broadcast relay. It receives encrypted patches, stores them, and forwards them to other connected clients via WebSocket. It never decrypts.
31	
32	4. **Snapshots + patch log** -- To avoid replaying the entire document history on load, periodic encrypted snapshots are stored. CryptPad creates a checkpoint every 50 patches. New clients load the latest snapshot and apply subsequent patches.
33	
34	### Ecosystem of Editor Technologies
35	
36	| Technology  | Type                           | Maturity                    | Encryption Support                | Notes                                                   |
37	| ----------- | ------------------------------ | --------------------------- | --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- |
38	| Yjs         | CRDT library                   | HIGH -- production at scale | Native -- designed for E2EE relay | Used by Skiff, Proton Docs (likely), many others        |
39	| Automerge   | CRDT library                   | HIGH                        | Compatible -- needs wrapper       | More academic, Rust core                                |
40	| ChainPad    | CRDT (blockchain-style)        | MEDIUM                      | Native -- built for CryptPad      | CryptPad-specific, not general purpose                  |
41	| SecSync     | E2EE CRDT relay protocol       | MEDIUM                      | Purpose-built for E2EE            | Built on top of Yjs, handles snapshot/update encryption |
42	| TipTap      | Rich text editor (ProseMirror) | HIGH                        | Via y-prosemirror binding         | Best-in-class rich text editing DX                      |
43	| ProseMirror | Low-level editor framework     | HIGH                        | Via y-prosemirror                 | More control, more work                                 |
44	| CodeMirror  | Code/text editor               | HIGH                        | Via y-codemirror                  | Better for code/markdown                                |
45	
46	**Recommendation:** Use **Yjs + SecSync + TipTap** for documents. Yjs is the industry standard CRDT for collaborative editing. SecSync provides the encrypted relay protocol (snapshots, updates, ephemeral messages) purpose-built for E2EE over Yjs. TipTap provides a rich ProseMirror-based editor with excellent Yjs integration via y-prosemirror.
47	
48	### Document Types and Their Complexity
49	
50	| Document Type        | Complexity  | Why                                                     | Recommendation                                           |
51	| -------------------- | ----------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- |
52	| Rich text (docs)     | HIGH        | Full formatting, images, tables, comments, suggestions  | Build first -- TipTap/ProseMirror + Yjs is well-trodden  |
53	| Markdown notes       | MEDIUM      | Simpler model, no complex formatting state              | Consider as simplified docs mode                         |
54	| Spreadsheets         | VERY HIGH   | Cell dependencies, formulas, cross-references, charting | Defer or use minimal grid -- Proton Sheets took years    |
55	| Slides/presentations | HIGH        | Layout engine, transitions, media, speaker notes        | Defer -- CryptPad does markdown-only slides for a reason |
56	| Whiteboard/drawing   | MEDIUM-HIGH | Canvas state, shape manipulation, infinite canvas       | Nice-to-have, not table stakes                           |
57	
58	**Key insight:** CryptPad, the most complete encrypted office suite, supports all these types but took a large team many years. Proton launched Docs in mid-2024 and Sheets in late 2025 -- over 18 months apart. Building even a basic encrypted rich text editor is a multi-month effort. Spreadsheets are an order of magnitude harder.
59	
60	### Table Stakes (Encrypted Docs)
61	
62	| Feature                            | Why Expected                                | Complexity | Depends On                                     |
63	| ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
64	| Real-time collaborative editing    | Core value proposition of any doc editor    | HIGH       | Yjs + WebSocket infrastructure                 |
65	| Cursor/selection awareness         | Users need to see where others are editing  | LOW        | Yjs awareness protocol (built-in)              |
66	| Basic rich text formatting         | Bold, italic, headings, lists, links        | MEDIUM     | TipTap extensions                              |
67	| Document-level encryption          | Each doc encrypted with unique key          | LOW        | Existing CipherBox encryption patterns         |
68	| Share document with specific users | Invite collaborators by re-wrapping doc key | MEDIUM     | M2 sharing infrastructure (ECIES key wrapping) |
69	| Offline single-user editing        | Edit without connection, sync when back     | MEDIUM     | Yjs persistence + SecSync reconciliation       |
70	| Document history/undo              | See past states, revert changes             | MEDIUM     | CRDT operation log + snapshots                 |
71	| Copy/paste from external sources   | Users paste from other apps constantly      | MEDIUM     | TipTap clipboard handling                      |
72	| Export to common formats           | PDF, Markdown, plain text export            | MEDIUM     | Client-side rendering + conversion             |
73	
74	### Differentiators (Encrypted Docs)
75	
76	| Feature                            | Value Proposition                                                                    | Complexity | Depends On                                  |
77	| ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------- | ------------------------------------------- |
78	| IPFS-backed document storage       | Documents stored on decentralized infrastructure, not corporate servers              | MEDIUM     | Existing IPFS infrastructure                |
79	| Verifiable document integrity      | IPFS CID proves document content has not been tampered with                          | LOW        | Native IPFS property                        |
80	| Client-side PDF export             | Generate PDF entirely in browser, never touches server                               | MEDIUM     | Browser PDF libraries (jsPDF, pdfmake)      |
81	| Encrypted comments and suggestions | Collaborative review workflow, all E2EE                                              | HIGH       | Additional Yjs doc for comment thread state |
82	| Encrypted image embedding          | Inline images encrypted and stored on IPFS                                           | HIGH       | Image upload + encryption + IPFS pinning    |
83	| TEE-backed document availability   | Documents remain accessible via TEE republishing even when all collaborators offline | LOW        | Existing TEE infrastructure from M1         |
84	
85	### Anti-Features (Encrypted Docs)
86	
87	| Anti-Feature                          | Why Avoid                                    | What to Do Instead                          |
88	| ------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
89	| Server-side document rendering        | Breaks zero-knowledge guarantee              | All rendering client-side only              |
90	| AI-powered writing assistance         | Requires server access to plaintext          | Skip entirely or run local-only models      |
91	| Full Google Docs feature parity       | Years of effort, not a tech demo goal        | Focus on clean, functional subset           |
92	| Complex table/formula support in docs | Massive complexity for edge-case use         | Basic tables only, no formulas in rich text |
93	| Real-time voice/video in editor       | Completely different infrastructure (WebRTC) | Out of scope, link to external tools        |
94	| Spell checking via server             | Sends plaintext to server                    | Browser-native spellcheck only              |
95	
96	---
97	
98	## Feature 2: Team Accounts
99	
100	### How It Works in Zero-Knowledge Systems
101	
102	The pattern, used by Tresorit, Cryptomator Hub, and Keeper, involves a key hierarchy:
103	
104	1. **Organization keypair** -- The org has a public/private key pair. The org private key is encrypted with the admin's personal key.
105	
106	2. **Team keypairs** -- Each team within the org has its own keypair. The team private key is encrypted with the org key (and optionally with each team member's personal key for redundancy).
107	
108	3. **Resource keys wrapped for teams** -- When a document or folder is shared with a team, the resource key is ECIES-wrapped for the team's public key. Any team member who can decrypt the team private key can then decrypt the resource key.
109	
110	4. **Role-based access** -- Roles control what actions a user can perform (admin, manager, editor, viewer). Crucially, roles are enforced at both the API level (server checks role before allowing action) and the crypto level (viewer might only receive read-only key material).
111	
112	5. **Device management** -- New devices generate a keypair; an existing trusted device re-encrypts the user's key material for the new device's public key. This is how Cryptomator Hub's Web of Trust model works.
113	
114	### Tresorit's Role Model (Industry Reference)
115	
116	| Role          | Capabilities                                             | Crypto Access                              |
117	| ------------- | -------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ |
118	| Owner (admin) | Full org management, billing, policy templates           | Org private key                            |
119	| Co-admin      | User management, policy, device management (not billing) | Org private key (subset)                   |
120	| Manager       | Share folders, manage permissions within scope           | Team private key                           |
121	| Editor        | Read, write, delete files/folders                        | Resource decryption keys                   |
122	| Viewer        | Read-only access                                         | Read-only key (if separate from write key) |
123

126	| Feature                                        | Why Expected                                          | Complexity | Depends On                                                 |
127	| ---------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------------------- |
128	| Create organization                            | User creates an org, becomes admin                    | MEDIUM     | New org entity in DB, org keypair generation               |
129	| Invite members by email/identifier             | Admin adds users to org                               | MEDIUM     | Invitation flow, key re-wrapping                           |
130	| Role-based permissions (admin, member, viewer) | Different access levels required                      | HIGH       | API enforcement + crypto-level key separation              |
131	| Team/group abstraction                         | Group users for bulk sharing                          | HIGH       | Team keypairs, membership management                       |
132	| Remove member with key revocation              | Must revoke access cryptographically, not just at API | VERY HIGH  | Key rotation for all resources the removed member accessed |

134	| Member device management                       | Admin can deauthorize lost devices                    | MEDIUM     | Device registry, key re-wrapping                           |

158	## Feature 3: Billing
159	
160	### How It Works for Privacy-Focused Products
161	
162	The challenge: accepting payment while preserving user privacy. Two tracks exist:
163	
164	**Track 1: Stripe (Traditional)**
165	Stripe handles subscription management, invoicing, payment method storage, and revenue recovery. Users provide card details. Not anonymous, but reliable. Stripe now supports stablecoin payments for subscriptions (launched October 2025) and Crypto.com integration (January 2026) for crypto-to-fiat conversion at checkout.
166	
167	**Track 2: Cryptocurrency (Privacy-Preserving)**
168	For users who want anonymity, accept crypto directly. Options:
169	
170	| Gateway       | Self-Hosted | Privacy Coins       | Recurring    | Complexity                       |
171	| ------------- | ----------- | ------------------- | ------------ | -------------------------------- |
172	| BTCPay Server | Yes (fully) | Yes (Monero plugin) | Manual       | HIGH -- self-host infrastructure |
173	| NOWPayments   | No (SaaS)   | Yes (300+ coins)    | Yes (native) | LOW -- API integration           |
174	| SHKeeper      | Yes         | Yes (Monero, LN)    | Partial      | MEDIUM                           |
175	| CoinGate      | No (SaaS)   | Limited             | Yes          | LOW                              |
176	
177	**Recommendation:** Use **Stripe** as primary billing for most users, and **BTCPay Server** (self-hosted) as the anonymous payment option. BTCPay Server supports Bitcoin, Lightning Network, and Monero via plugins. It is the gold standard for self-hosted, censorship-resistant payment processing with no intermediary fees and no KYC requirements for the merchant. NOWPayments is an acceptable alternative if self-hosting BTCPay Server is too much operational overhead for a demo.
178

182	| -------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | ---------- | ---------------------------------- |

184	| Paid subscription tiers                            | Unlock more storage, features      | MEDIUM     | Stripe Billing integration         |
185	| Stripe Checkout / Payment Links                    | Standard card payment flow         | LOW        | Stripe SDK, webhook handler        |
186	| Subscription management (upgrade/downgrade/cancel) | Self-service billing               | MEDIUM     | Stripe Customer Portal             |
187	| Invoices and receipts                              | Legal/tax requirement              | LOW        | Stripe auto-generates these        |
188	| Webhook-driven provisioning                        | Grant access when payment succeeds | MEDIUM     | Webhook handler + DB updates       |
189	| Grace period for failed payments                   | Don't lock out users instantly     | LOW        | Stripe Smart Retries + grace logic |

213	
214	## Feature 4: Secure Document Signing
215	
216	### How It Works in Zero-Knowledge Systems
217	
218	Document signing in an encrypted context follows this flow:
219	
220	1. **Document is decrypted client-side** -- The signer decrypts the document locally.
221	2. **Signer reviews plaintext** -- Signer sees the document content in their browser.
222	3. **Signature is computed client-side** -- Using the signer's private key (secp256k1 from Web3Auth), the client computes a digital signature over the document hash.
223	4. **Signature is stored alongside document** -- The signature (plus signer's public key and timestamp) is stored as metadata, encrypted and pinned to IPFS.
224	5. **Anyone with access can verify** -- Given the document, the signature, and the signer's public key, verification is a standard ECDSA verify operation.
225	
226	This is fundamentally different from DocuSign/Adobe Sign where the server orchestrates signing. In a ZK system, the server never sees the document, so all signing must be client-side.
227	
228	### Types of Signatures
229	
230	| Type                                 | Legal Weight                      | Implementation                  | Complexity   |
231	| ------------------------------------ | --------------------------------- | ------------------------------- | ------------ |
232	| Electronic signature (drawn/typed)   | LOW -- intent to sign             | Canvas capture, stored as image | LOW          |
233	| Cryptographic digital signature      | HIGH -- mathematically verifiable | ECDSA sign over document hash   | MEDIUM       |
234	| Qualified electronic signature (QES) | HIGHEST -- EU eIDAS compliant     | Requires certified hardware/CA  | OUT OF SCOPE |
235	
236	**Recommendation:** Implement **cryptographic digital signatures** using the existing secp256k1 keys from Web3Auth. This is a natural fit -- every CipherBox user already has a key pair. The signature is mathematically verifiable and tied to the user's identity. Add a visual signature capture (drawn/typed) as a UX layer on top.
237	
238	### Table Stakes (Document Signing)
239	
240	| Feature                              | Why Expected                          | Complexity | Depends On                                 |
241	| ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------- | ---------- | ------------------------------------------ |
242	| Sign document with private key       | Core signing action                   | MEDIUM     | Web3Auth key access, ECDSA signing         |
243	| Verify signature given public key    | Anyone can check signature validity   | LOW        | Standard ECDSA verify                      |
244	| Visual signature capture (draw/type) | Users expect to "draw" a signature    | LOW        | HTML Canvas signature pad                  |
245	| Multi-party signing workflow         | Send document for multiple signers    | HIGH       | Signing state machine, notification system |
246	| Audit trail of signing events        | Who signed what and when              | MEDIUM     | Append-only signed log                     |
247	| Signed document export (PDF)         | Produce a signed PDF for external use | HIGH       | PDF generation with embedded signature     |
248	
249	### Differentiators (Document Signing)
250	
251	| Feature                             | Value Proposition                                                        | Complexity | Depends On                                     |
252	| ----------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------- |
253	| IPFS-anchored signatures            | Signature stored on decentralized infrastructure, tamper-evident via CID | LOW        | Existing IPFS infrastructure                   |
254	| Blockchain-timestamped signing      | Anchor document hash on-chain for irrefutable timestamp proof            | MEDIUM     | Smart contract or Bitcoin OP_RETURN            |
255	| Zero-knowledge signing verification | Verify a signature exists without revealing document content             | VERY HIGH  | ZK-SNARK circuits                              |
256	| Client-side PDF signing (PKCS#7)    | Generate industry-standard signed PDFs entirely in browser               | HIGH       | node-signpdf or similar, X.509 cert generation |
257	| Signature request workflow          | Request signatures from other CipherBox users                            | MEDIUM     | Notification system, signing queue             |
258	
259	### Anti-Features (Document Signing)
260	
261	| Anti-Feature                                 | Why Avoid                                               | What to Do Instead                                          |
262	| -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- |
263	| eIDAS/QES compliance                         | Requires certified CA, hardware tokens, legal framework | Cryptographic signatures with optional blockchain anchoring |
264	| Server-side signing                          | Breaks ZK guarantee                                     | All signing is client-side                                  |
265	| Notarization service                         | Legal complexity, jurisdiction-dependent                | Provide timestamping as building block                      |
266	| Signing for non-CipherBox users              | Requires external key management                        | Limit to CipherBox-to-CipherBox signing                     |
267	| Template-based signing (fill-and-sign forms) | DocuSeal territory, massive scope                       | Simple "sign this document" flow only                       |
